=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a permutation P=(P_1,P_2,\dots,P_N) of (1,2,\dots,N).
Consider the following operations k\ (k=2,3,\dots,N) on this permutation.

- Operation k: For i=1,2,\dots,k-1 in this order, if P_i > P_{i+1}, swap the values of the i-th and (i+1)-th elements of P.

You are also given a non-decreasing sequence A=(A_1,A_2,\dots,A_M)\ (2 \leq A_i \leq N) of length M.
For each i=1,2,\dots,M, find the inversion number of P after applying the operations A_1, A_2, \dots, A_i in this order.

 What is the inversion number of a sequence?

The inversion number of a sequence x=(x_1,x_2,\dots,x_n) of length n is the number of pairs of integers (i,j)\ (1\leq i < j \leq n) such that x_i > x_j.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
P_1 P_2 \dots P_N
M
A_1 A_2 \dots A_M

Output

Print M lines. The k-th line should contain the answer to the problem for i=k.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq M \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 2 \leq A_i \leq N
- P is a permutation of (1,2,\dots,N).
- A_i \leq A_{i+1} for i=1,2,\dots,M-1.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
3 2 4 1 6 5
2
4 6

Sample Output 1

3
1

First, operation 4 is performed. During this, P changes as follows: (3,2,4,1,6,5) \rightarrow (2,3,4,1,6,5) \rightarrow (2,3,4,1,6,5) \rightarrow (2,3,1,4,6,5). The inversion number of P afterward is 3.
Next, operation 6 is performed, where P eventually becomes (2,1,3,4,5,6), whose inversion number is 1.

Sample Input 2

20
12 14 16 8 7 15 19 6 18 5 13 9 10 17 4 1 11 20 2 3
15
3 4 6 8 8 9 10 12 13 15 18 18 19 19 20

Sample Output 2

117
116
113
110
108
105
103
99
94
87
79
72
65
58
51

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Each operation k is one pass of bubble sort on prefix. How does inversion count change? In a bubble sort pass, each swap reduces inversion count by exactly 1 (since swapping adjacent inverted pair). Maybe there is a formula for number of swaps in operation k in terms of "descents" or "inversions crossing position k"? Let's examine.

In one left-to-right bubble pass on prefix k, each swap removes an inversion between adjacent elements. Which inversions are removed? An inversion pair (a,b) with a before b and a>b. In a bubble pass, if they become adjacent and a still before b, they swap. After swap, their relative order becomes correct (b before a), and they will never swap back in same pass because a moves right, b moves left; they separate. So each inversion pair is swapped at most once per pass. The number of swaps equals number of inversion pairs that are "resolved" during that pass. Which inversions are resolved? In one pass, an element can move right across all smaller elements that are to its right until blocked by larger. So a large element a swaps with every smaller element to its right that is before the next larger element? Actually if a is larger than b and b is to right, they will swap if a catches b before a is blocked by an even larger element. In a left-to-right pass, an element a moves right as long as next element is smaller. It stops when next element is larger. Thus it swaps with a contiguous block of smaller elements immediately to its right at the time it is processed. Elements to its right that are separated by a larger element won't be swapped with a in this pass. So swaps correspond to adjacent "runs" of decreasing? Let's characterize.

At start of operation k, consider the prefix. The pass scans i=1..k-1. At each edge, if left>right swap. This is exactly one pass of bubble sort. The number of swaps equals the number of descents in the "inversion graph"? So operation k on prefix is: Let records (left-to-right maxima) in current prefix P_1..P_k. Partition the prefix into blocks, each block starts at a record and ends just before the next record (last block ends at k). In each block, cyclically shift the block left by one (i.e., move the first element (the record) to the end of the block). This is elegant.

Equivalently, each left-to-right maximum (record) moves right to just before the next record (or to k if last), and all other elements in its block shift left by one. This matches particle: records move right across the non-record elements in their block; non-records shift left.

But there is another issue: The operation "remove all elements in [2,k]" and "insert k" with possible duplicate 1. We can implement:

Let's design treap operations.

We need a treap that supports:
- split(root, key): returns (a,b) where a contains keys ≤ key, b contains keys > key. (Or < key? We'll use ≤)
- merge(a,b): assumes all keys in a < all keys in b (or ≤? no duplicates).
- apply_add(node, delta): adds delta to all keys in subtree.
- push(node): propagate lazy_add to children and node.key.
- update(node): maintain size.

Node fields: key (int), priority (random), left, right, size, lazy (int).

Because keys can be modified by lazy, the BST invariant is by current key values. When we apply add to a whole subtree, all keys shift by same amount, so relative order within subtree unchanged. When merging, we must ensure all keys in left < all keys in right. Our merge order ensures that.

But wait: If we apply lazy_add -1 to M, and M contains keys 2..k. After shift, keys 1..k-1. L contains key 1. If M contains key 1 after shift, we split off ≤1 and discard. Then M2 keys are >1 and ≤k-1. L key=1 < M2. Then insert k. R keys >k. So merge order valid.

Need ensure split by key works correctly with lazy tags. Standard split:
```
def split(t, key):
    if not t: return (None,None)
    push(t)
    if t.key <= key:
        a, b = split(t.right, key)
        t.right = a
        upd(t)
        return t, b
    else:
        a, b = split(t.left, key)
        t.left = b
        upd(t)
        return a, t
```
This assumes left subtree keys < t.key < right subtree keys. With lazy tags, push ensures t.key and children's lazy updated. But children's keys may not be physically updated until pushed; however their lazy tags account. The split recursion will push as needed. This is standard.

apply_add:
```
def apply(t, delta):
    if t:
        t.key += delta
        t.lazy += delta
```
But if we add delta to t.key and t.lazy, then children's keys are not updated yet; t.lazy indicates children need delta. When push(t), apply delta to children and clear. This works.

However, there is a subtlety: If we call apply_add on M, we add -1 to root.key and root.lazy. The root.key changes, but its left/right children keys not yet. The BST invariant with root.key? Since all keys in M shift by -1, root.key should also shift. Children will shift when pushed. The relative order remains. But if we then split M by key 1, split will push root, applying -1 to children, so keys become correct. Good.

Need initialize treap with S positions. Since S sorted, we can build treap in O(N) by stack or just insert one by one O(N log N) acceptable 2e5. But Python recursion? Need set recursionlimit high. Treap operations recursive depth expected O(log N), but random priorities; set recursionlimit 1e6.

Need compute initial inversion number inv0. N up to 2e5, P permutation. Use Fenwick tree O(N log N): iterate values? Standard: inv = sum over i of count of previous elements greater than P_i. Use BIT over values: for each x in P, inv += i-1 - bit.sum(x); bit.add(x,1). Or iterate positions.

Need compute initial S: scan P left to right, maintain max; if P_i > max, S.add(i), max=P_i. Since first always record. Note record positions are positions, not values.

Now, is the formula for swaps = k - R_k always valid for operation k on current permutation? We proved via block rotation. Edge cases:
- k=2. Operation 2: compare first two, swap if P1>P2. Records in prefix2: R_2 = 1 if P1>P2? Actually if P1>P2, records: pos1 only (since P2 not record) => R=1. k-R=1 swap. If P1<P2, records: pos1,pos2 => R=2, k-R=0. Good. Update S with k=2:

- k=N. Operation N on full array. R_N = total records in S. Swaps = N - R_N. Is that always number of swaps in one full bubble pass? For a permutation, number of swaps in one bubble sort pass equals N - (# left-to-right maxima)? Let's test [3,1,4,2,5], records=3, N=5, swaps=2 yes. [2,3,1,4], records=3, N=4, swaps=1 yes. [5,1,2,3,4], records=1 (5), N=5, swaps=4? Pass: 5>1,5>2,5>3,5>4 =4 yes. [2,1,4,3], records:2,4 =>2, N=4, swaps? Pass: 2>1 swap, 2<4 no, 4>3 swap =>2. Yes. Known: one bubble pass swaps N - #left-to-right maxima. Good.

Now, does the record position update formula hold for any operation k, even if k is not a record before? We derived from block rotation. Let's test with random [2,1,4,3], S={1,3}. Operation k=3. R=2, swaps=1. Actual op3 on [2,1,4,3]: i1 2>1 swap [1,2,4,3]; i2 2<4 no => [1,2,4,3]. Inv decrease1. S after: records [1,2,4] positions {1,2,3}? Operation k=4 next on [1,2,4,3], S={1,2,3}, R=3, swaps=1. Actual op4: 1<2 no,2<4 no,4>3 swap => [1,2,3,4], swaps1. Update S with k=4: T={2,3} -> {1,2}; plus {1},{4} => {1,2,4}. Output records [1,2,4] positions {1,2,4}. Yes.

Now, the sequence A is non-decreasing. Does our update require A non-decreasing? The block rotation formula for operation k applies regardless of previous operations. The record position update formula applies regardless. So even if A not sorted, it would work? Operation k only depends on current S, no need non-decreasing. But constraints have non-decreasing maybe for other reasons? Our treap simulation would work for any sequence, as long as we process in order. So fine.

Need be careful: The record positions S are global records. Operation k affects only prefix k. We assumed records >k unchanged. Is that always true? Operation k doesn't change elements after k, and the maximum of prefix k remains the same value (the global max of prefix k) after operation, because block rotations preserve multiset and the maximum ends at position k. Thus for any position j>k, the max of P_1..P_{j-1} after operation is max( old max of prefix k, old max of positions k+1..j-1 ). Since old max of prefix k is same value, and positions k+1..j-1 unchanged, the record status of j unchanged. Good. Also no new records appear after k due to changed order within prefix? Record status depends only on maximum value before, not order, so unchanged. Thus S update only within ≤k.

Now, inversion count decrease = number of swaps = k - R_k. We don't need to update inv by actual swaps? Each swap reduces inversion count by exactly 1. Since operation k performs exactly k - R_k swaps. Need prove number of swaps = k - R_k. From block rotation, each block of length L is left-rotated by moving the record (which is larger than all elements in its block) from first to last. This requires L-1 adjacent swaps? Wait operation k is a sequence of compare-exchanges; the block rotation is the net effect. Does the net effect correspond exactly to L-1 swaps per block, no extra swaps that cancel? In a compare-exchange pass, each swap reduces inversion count by 1; the net permutation is block rotations. The number of swaps performed equals the number of inversions removed. We can also count swaps directly from block rotation: The record at front of each block swaps with each subsequent element in the block exactly once, moving to the end. Since all elements in block are smaller than record, each comparison with next element results in swap. Thus total swaps = sum(L_s - 1) = k - t = k - R_k. This is a direct count of swaps in the actual pass, not just net. Need ensure the pass indeed processes exactly those swaps and no others. The block rotation characterization was derived from T_k recurrence; it also implies the sequence of swaps is exactly moving each record to end of block. Let's verify with [3,1,4,2,5]: blocks [3,1] and [4,2] and [5]; swaps: 3 with1, 4 with2 =2. Yes. So swaps count = k - R_k.

Now, implementation details for treap.

Let's design functions:

```
import random, sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000)

class Node:
    __slots__ = ('key','prio','left','right','size','lazy')
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.prio = random.randrange(1<<30)
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.size = 1
        self.lazy = 0
```

Random priorities: using random module may be slow but 2e5 nodes okay. Could use deterministic xorshift for speed. But Python random okay? 2e5 operations, each creates node, random calls ~2e5, fine. But recursion with random priorities expected O(log N). Need avoid worst-case if random bad; use random.getrandbits.

```
def sz(t): return t.size if t else 0
def upd(t):
    if t:
        t.size = 1 + sz(t.left) + sz(t.right)
def apply(t, delta):
    if t:
        t.key += delta
        t.lazy += delta
def push(t):
    if t and t.lazy:
        d = t.lazy
        if t.left: apply(t.left, d)
        if t.right: apply(t.right, d)
        t.lazy = 0
```

Wait: apply(t, delta) increments t.key and t.lazy. If we call apply on root M with delta=-1, root.key becomes key-1, root.lazy += -1. Then push(root) will apply -1 to children and clear root.lazy. But root.key already updated; children keys updated then. Good. However, if root.lazy was nonzero before, apply adds delta; push applies total. Good.

But there is a subtle bug: In apply(t, delta), if we increment t.key, and later push, we apply delta to children. But what about t.key when t.lazy was nonzero? Suppose t.lazy=5 (children need +5, t.key already +5). apply(t,-1): t.key += -1 (now +4 relative to original? Actually original key +5 then -1 = +4), t.lazy += -1 (5-1=4). push applies 4 to children, t.key remains +4. Correct.

Split:
```
def split(t, key):
    # returns (a,b): a keys <= key, b keys > key
    if not t:
        return None, None
    push(t)
    if t.key <= key:
        a, b = split(t.right, key)
        t.right = a
        upd(t)
        return t, b
    else:
        a, b = split(t.left, key)
        t.left = b
        upd(t)
        return a, t
```
Need ensure if t.right has lazy, split pushes as needed. Good.

Merge:
```
def merge(a,b):
    if not a or not b: return a or b
    if a.prio < b.prio: # min heap? Use higher priority root
        push(a)
        a.right = merge(a.right, b)
        upd(a)
        return a
    else:
        push(b)
        b.left = merge(a, b.left)
        upd(b)
        return b
```
Priorities: random; if smaller priority root (min-heap). Either.

Build initial treap from sorted S. We can just insert each position:
```
root = None
for pos in S:
    root = merge(root, Node(pos))
```
This is O(N log N) with random priorities. 2e5 okay. But merge sequentially with random priorities expected O(log N) each, fine. Could build O(N) by Cartesian stack for speed, but not necessary. However, Python recursion in merge for each insert maybe okay 2e5*log. But random priorities and recursion depth maybe ~50. Fine.

But there is a potential issue: If we insert sequentially using merge, and priorities are random, the treap shape is random. Good.

Now process operations:
```
inv = initial_inv
root = build(S)
out = []
for k in A:
    # Count R_k = size of S <= k
    left, right = split(root, k)
    R = sz(left)
    root = merge(left, right)
    inv -= (k - R)
    out.append(str(inv))
    # Update S
    # Split into L <=1, M 2..k, R >k
    L, rest = split(root, 1)
    M, Rpart = split(rest, k)  # M keys 2..k (since rest >1)
    # Apply -1 to M
    apply(M, -1)
    # Remove any key <=1 from M (duplicate 1)
    M1, M2 = split(M, 1)
    # M1 discarded (should be key 1 if existed)
    # Insert k
    node_k = Node(k)
    root = merge(merge(merge(L, M2), node_k), Rpart)
```
Wait, after we split root for count, we merged back. Then split again. Could combine to avoid double split, but fine O(log N) twice per op. M operations 2e5, okay. But note after count split/merge, root is same. Then update split.

However, there is a subtle issue: The count query split(root,k) returns left with keys ≤k. But if we then merge left,right, the treap is restored. Good.

But when we split for update: split(root,1) returns L (keys ≤1) and rest (>1). Since keys positive and 1 always present, L size=1. Then split(rest,k) returns M (keys ≤k, i.e., 2..k) and Rpart (>k). Good.

After apply(M,-1), M keys are in [1,k-1]. Split(M,1) returns M1 (≤1) and M2 (>1). M1 may contain key 1 if original M had 2. We discard M1. But wait, if original M did not have 2, M after -1 has min ≥2? Actually if original min=3, after -1 min=2, so M1 empty. Good.

Then merge L (key 1) + M2 + node_k + Rpart. Need ensure M2 keys are >1 and ≤k-1, so L < M2 < k < Rpart. Yes.

But what if L is None? Should not, but if root somehow no 1, L None; then M1 might contain 1 and we discarded, losing 1. To be safe, we can ensure 1 is inserted if L is None or if M1 had 1. But initial and updates always keep 1. Let's prove: Initial S contains 1. Update: L contains 1; we never remove L; M1 discarded only from M, not L. So L always non-None. Good.

Now, one more subtlety: The count query split(root,k) before update uses current S. But if k is large, left includes all records ≤k. R_k = sz(left). Then merge back. Good.

Let's test update with sample:

Now, need compute initial inversion number. Use BIT:
```
bit = [0]*(N+1)
def add(i,v):
    while i<=N: bit[i]+=v; i+=i&-i
def sum(i):
    s=0
    while i>0: s+=bit[i]; i-=i&-i
    return s
inv=0
for idx,x in enumerate(P, start=1):
    inv += (idx-1) - sum(x)
    add(x,1)
```
Since permutation values 1..N. This counts previous elements greater than x. Good.

Initial S:
```
S=[]
mx=0
for i,x in enumerate(P,1):
    if x>mx:
        S.append(i)
        mx=x
```
Because P_i values distinct; first always.

Now, performance. Treap operations per A_i: count split+merge (2 splits? split once, merge once), update split+split+split+merge+merge+merge+merge? Let's count:
- count: split(root,k) -> 1 split, 1 merge.
- update: split(root,1), split(rest,k), apply, split(M,1), then merge L,M2, merge with node, merge with Rpart: 3 merges? Actually merge(merge(merge(L,M2), node), Rpart) = 3 merge calls. Total per op: 3 splits + 4 merges. Each O(log N). M=2e5, ~7*log(2e5) ~ 7*18=126 recursive operations per op, 25 million recursive calls, maybe okay in Python if optimized? Need be careful. We can reduce splits/merges.

We can combine count and update in one set of splits:
- We need R_k = size of S ≤ k before update.
- For update, we need L (≤1), M (2..k), R (>k).
We can do:
```
L, rest = split(root, 1)
M, Rpart = split(rest, k)
R_k = 1 + sz(M)  # since L is {1}
```
Wait R_k = count of records ≤k = size(L) + size(M). L size should be 1. But if we don't know, use sz(L)+sz(M). This gives count before update. Then we don't need separate count split. We can perform update using these same parts. Great! Because update also needs L, M, Rpart. So per operation:
- split(root,1) -> L, rest
- split(rest,k) -> M, Rpart
- R = sz(L)+sz(M)
- inv -= k - R
- apply(M,-1)
- split(M,1) -> M1, M2 (discard M1)
- root = merge(merge(merge(L, M2), Node(k)), Rpart)
That's 3 splits + 3 merges per op. Good.

Can we avoid split(M,1) by checking if key 2 in M? We could but split is fine. Maybe we can handle duplicate 1 differently: Since L contains 1, and M after shift may contain 1. If we merge L and M directly, there would be duplicate key 1, violating BST uniqueness. We could instead when applying -1 to M, if M contains 2, we need remove one 1. Now, can we further optimize by using a Fenwick tree with "shifted indices" instead of treap? Let's consider if there is an even simpler data structure due to non-decreasing A. The update shifts all records in [2,k] down by 1 and inserts k. This is reminiscent of maintaining a set under "cut and paste": remove a prefix of the sorted list (positions ≤k), decrement all, insert k. Since A non-decreasing, k increases over time. Maybe we can process with a queue? Let's explore to see if simpler/faster.

S is a set of record positions. Operation k:
- Shift all elements ≤k (except 1) down by 1.
- Insert k.
This means every time we perform an operation with a new larger k, all existing records except 1 that are ≤k get shifted left by 1. Since k is non-decreasing, the set of records affected grows. Maybe we can maintain records in a list with an offset? Let's examine.

Let S sorted: s_1=1 < s_2 < ... < s_t. Operation k affects records with s_i ≤ k. For each affected s_i (i≥2), new position s_i -1. Then insert k. This is like: all records in a prefix of the sorted list are decremented by 1; then k appended (if k > all affected? k is ≥ all affected? Since affected are ≤k, and k is inserted; after decrement, affected ≤k-1, so k becomes the largest among affected and maybe before unaffected >k). Since A non-decreasing, k increases, so the "threshold" k in terms of position is non-decreasing. But the positions of records shift left, so the set of records ≤k may not simply grow; some records may move below k, and k inserted. Maybe we can maintain S in a data structure with a global offset for a prefix of the list.

Let's look at the update in terms of sorted list. Suppose S = [1, s_2, s_3, ..., s_t]. Let p = number of records with position ≤k. Affected are indices 2..p (since s_1=1). New list:
- 1 remains.
- For i=2..p: s_i -1.
- k inserted.
- Records i=p+1..t remain (positions >k).
But note s_i -1 may not be sorted relative to 1 and each other; they are sorted because s_i increasing. k is > s_p -1? Since s_p ≤ k, s_p -1 ≤ k-1 < k. And k < s_{p+1}? Since s_{p+1} > k by definition of p. So order: 1, s_2-1, s_3-1, ..., s_p-1, k, s_{p+1},...
Thus the sorted list transformation is:
Take the sublist of records from index 2 to p, subtract 1 from each, and insert k between this sublist and the rest.
This is like: remove the first element 1? Actually 1 stays at front. The sublist shifts left by 1 in value, but its length same. Then insert k after it.

Since k is non-decreasing, p (number of records ≤k) maybe non-decreasing? Let's check. After update, record positions shift left, so more records may become ≤ next k. p can increase. But k itself increases. Could p ever decrease? The number of records ≤k after update? S_new has 1, shifted sublist (values ≤k-1), k, and old >k. So all shifted sublist are ≤k-1 < k, plus k, so there are p records ≤k? Actually old records ≤k: 1 + (p-1) = p. New records ≤k: 1 + (p-1 shifted) + k = p+1? Wait shifted sublist has p-1 elements all ≤k-1, plus 1, plus k => p+1 records ≤k. So count of records ≤k increases by 1 every operation? But if duplicate 1? We discarded duplicate, but shifted sublist may include 1 if s_2=2, then p-1 shifted includes 1 duplicate with 1; after discarding, count ≤k is 1 + (p-2) + 1 = p? Let's compute. If s_2=2, shifted sublist has p-1 elements, one of which is 1; after discard, p-2 elements >1, plus 1, plus k => p records ≤k. So count ≤k increases by 1 if s_2>2, else same. This matches record count increase. But p for next operation with k' ≥ k? Since k increases, count of records ≤k' likely non-decreasing? Not necessarily but probably.

Could we maintain S as a list and use a "lazy offset" for a prefix? Since update subtracts 1 from a contiguous range of the sorted list (indices 2..p) and inserts k. This is a list operation: replace a contiguous segment by its values minus 1, and insert k after it. If we store values with a lazy offset for the segment, we need split list by index, apply offset, insert. This is exactly an implicit treap where keys are not used for ordering? Wait the order of S is by position, and update preserves order: [1, s_2-1, ..., s_p-1, k, s_{p+1},...]. This is a list operation on the sorted list. We don't actually need to split by key; we need to find p = number of elements ≤k. But if we maintain an implicit treap (order by list index) with values as positions, we can find p by searching value ≤k (requires values). Then apply offset to a range of indices (2..p), insert k at position p+1. This is similar but maybe easier: implicit treap with lazy add to values for a range of nodes, and split by index. However, we still need to find p (last index with value ≤k). We can do that by walking the treap using values, with lazy propagation. That's essentially the same as keyed treap but split by key. The keyed treap already handles.

Maybe there is a simpler "Fenwick with range add and point query" if we represent S as a binary array of length N (1 if record position). Operation k:
- For all positions x in [2,k] with bit=1, move bit from x to x-1.
- Set bit[k]=1.
This is like: shift the bit pattern in [1,k] right? Actually bits at positions 2..k move to 1..k-1, and bit k becomes 1. This is equivalent to: For the prefix [1,k], the new bit array is: bit[1]=1, for i=2..k: new_bit[i] = old_bit[i+1]? Wait old bit at x moves to x-1, so new_bit[y] = old_bit[y+1] for y=1..k-1, and new_bit[k]=1. Also old bit1=1 remains, but if old_bit2=1 then new_bit1=1 anyway. So for prefix [1,k], the bit array becomes:
new[1] = 1
new[i] = old[i+1] for 2 ≤ i ≤ k-1
new[k] = 1
and for i>k unchanged.
This is a simple operation on a binary string: shift the prefix bits [2..k] left by one? Actually old bits [2..k] become new bits [1..k-1], and new bit k=1. So the prefix length k is transformed by taking old bits 2..k, shifting them one position left, and placing 1 at the end. This is exactly a left rotation of the bit string of length k? Let's see old prefix bits b[1..k]. New prefix bits:
new[1] = 1 (but if old b2=1, new1=1; if old b2=0, new1=1 from old b1? Wait formula new[1]=old[2] if old[2] exists? Let's derive from set update: new positions = {1} ∪ {x-1 | x∈S∩[2,k]} ∪ {k}. So for y=1: new bit = 1 OR (old bit2). Since old bit1=1, but old bit1 doesn't map to new1 unless old bit2? Actually old bit1 remains at 1, so new1=1 regardless. Thus new[1]=1.
For 2≤y≤k-1: new[y] = old[y+1] (because x=y+1 maps to y).
For y=k: new[k]=1.
So new prefix = [1, old2, old3, ..., old_k? Wait old[y+1] for y=2..k-1 gives old3..old_k. So new = [1, old2? no for y=2 new=old3; y=3 new=old4; ... y=k-1 new=old_k; y=k=1]. Let's list:
new[1]=1

For S={1,3,5}, k=4: old bits [1,0,1,0,1]; new prefix length4 = [1] + old[3..4] + [1] = [1,1,0,1] -> positions 1,2,4. Wait S_new {1,2,4} yes. But earlier we had {1,2,4,5}; for prefix4 bits [1,1,0,1]. Let's analyze operation k more thoroughly.

We have current permutation. Records in prefix k: r_1=1 < r_2 < ... < r_t ≤ k. Blocks B_s = positions [r_s, r_{s+1}-1] for s<t, B_t = [r_t, k]. Each block starts with record value v_s = P_{r_s}. Records values increasing: v_1 < v_2 < ... < v_t (since records are left-to-right maxima). All other elements in block s are less than v_s (because v_s is record, and until next record no element exceeds v_s). Operation k left-rotates each block:
- New first element of block s is the second element of old block s, if length ≥2. Let's denote w_s = P_{r_s+1} if r_s+1 ≤ r_{s+1}-1 (or ≤k for last), else no second element. Since w_s < v_s.
- Old record v_s moves to end of block (position r_{s+1}-1 for s<t, or k for s=t).

After operation, the output sequence is concatenation of rotated blocks. What are the new records?
Let's denote for each block s:
If length L_s = r_{s+1}-r_s (for s<t) or k-r_t+1 (for s=t).
If L_s = 1, block is just [v_s], remains [v_s].
If L_s ≥2, block becomes [w_s, (other elements), v_s].

The record status in the output:
- At the very beginning, the first element of block1 (which is w_1 if L_1≥2, else v_1) is a record.
- Then within each block, the old record v_s at the end is always a record? Need compare with previous elements. The previous element before v_s in the output is the last element of the rotated block s, which is the element just before v_s in original block (if L_s≥2) or if L_s=1, v_s itself. Since all elements in block s are < v_s, v_s is greater than all elements in its block. Also previous records v_1..v_{s-1} are < v_s. So v_s is a record. Yes.
- Additionally, the first element of block s+1 (w_{s+1} if L_{s+1}≥2) may be a record if it is greater than the maximum of all previous output elements. The maximum before block s+1 starts is v_s (the old record at end of block s) because records increasing and v_s > all previous. Thus w_{s+1} is a record iff w_{s+1} > v_s. If L_{s+1}=1, its first element is v_{s+1} > v_s, so it is a record (as old record). Actually if L_{s+1}=1, the block is just v_{s+1}, which is a record anyway. So we can say: For each block s≥2, the first element of the rotated block (which is w_s if L_s≥2, else v_s) is a record iff that element > v_{s-1}. If L_s=1, element = v_s > v_{s-1}, so yes. If L_s≥2, w_s may or may not > v_{s-1}. For block1, first element is always record.

- Position 1 (start of block1).
- For each old record v_s (s=1..t), its new position (end of block s) is a record.
- For each block s≥2 with L_s≥2, if w_s > v_{s-1}, then the start position of block s (which is r_s after shift? Let's compute positions) is also a record.

This depends on comparisons between w_s (element after record r_s) and previous record v_{s-1}. So record positions alone insufficient. Need maintain more info.

Maybe operation k corresponds to decreasing the inversion vector of values ≤? Let's compute with block rotation. In each block, record v_s moves to end, crossing all elements in its block. Original blocks: block s contains record v_s and non-records all < v_s. Since records increasing v_1<...<v_t. Block boundaries are record positions r_s. Operation k left-rotates each block, so block s occupies same positions as before? Original block s positions r_s..r_{s+1}-1. After left rotation, the elements of block s are still in those positions (just rotated). Yes. So cross-block inversions remain exactly the same. Within each block, the only change is moving the record v_s from first to last, crossing all other elements in the block. Since v_s is larger than all other elements in the block, every crossing removes an inversion between v_s and that element. The relative order of the other elements is unchanged, so their mutual inversions unchanged. Therefore the inversion count decrease is exactly sum over blocks of (L_s - 1) = k - R_k, and the only inversions removed are those between each record and the non-record elements in its block. This is a crucial insight.

Moreover, the state of inversions can be represented by blocks. Operation k removes all inversions between records and elements in their own block. Can we maintain blocks in a data structure that supports:
- For prefix k, identify blocks (records) and their lengths.
- Rotate each block left by one (i.e., move first element to end).
- Update record set: old records remain records; additionally, for each block s≥2, if its first element after rotation (which is old second element) > previous record, then that position becomes a record, splitting? Actually if it becomes a record, then the block decomposition changes: the new record at start of block s means the previous block? Let's see.

After operation, old records remain records. New records may appear at the start of blocks s≥2. If a new record appears at position r_s (start of block s), then in the new permutation, the record sequence includes ... v_{s-1} at end of block s-1, then new record w_s at position r_s, then ... v_s at end of block s. So the block s is split? The new record w_s is at the start of old block s, and old record v_s is at the end of old block s. Between them are the other elements of old block s. So the old block s is now split into two blocks: block starting at w_s (length? until v_s) and block starting at v_s (until next old record). If w_s ≤ v_{s-1}, then w_s is not a record, so old block s starts with a non-record; but the next record is v_s at the end of the block. Thus the block decomposition after operation is not simply old blocks; blocks may start at new records inside old blocks.

Alternative: Maybe we can simulate the actual permutation using a balanced binary tree that supports operation k in O(log N) amortized using "small to large"? Let's think about constraints and possible total complexity.

Maybe we can maintain the permutation as a collection of blocks with lazy rotations. Operation k rotates all blocks in prefix k. This is like applying a cyclic shift to each block. So blocks can merge/split in complex ways.

This suggests maintaining block decomposition explicitly is hard.

But there is a known "bubble sort pass" in terms of inversion table: After one pass, the inversion count decreases by N - records. After c passes, the inversion count decreases by sum_{i=1}^c (N - R_i), where R_i is records before pass i. Not helpful.

Given time, maybe we should search for another approach.

Let's consider using a treap that stores the actual permutation and supports operation k by applying a "lazy rotation" to blocks. We can represent the permutation as a sequence of "runs" where each run is a block that will be rotated. But we saw block decomposition changes. However, maybe we can use a splay tree with "reverse" lazy? Operation k left-rotates each block. This is equivalent to: For each record position, move the record to the end of its block. If we maintain the permutation in a treap and also maintain the set of record positions, we can perform operation k by:
- For each record in prefix k, move the node at that position to the end of its block.
Moving a record to the end of its block is a cut-and-paste: remove the record from its position and insert it just before the next record (or at k). If we do this for all records, the total number of records moved could be large. But maybe we can do it lazily: all records in prefix k are moved to the end of their blocks simultaneously. This is like applying a permutation to the positions of records. If we maintain the sequence of records and non-records separately, operation k might be a simple rotation of the record list?

Let's separate the permutation into records (R) and non-records (N). In a prefix, the sequence is R N* R N* ... R N* (each block starts with R followed by some Ns). Operation k left-rotates each block: the R at the start moves to the end of its block, so the block becomes N* R. Thus the pattern of R and N within each block is reversed: R followed by Ns becomes Ns followed by R. The Ns are in the same relative order. So after operation, the prefix is a sequence of blocks each of form N* R. The old R's are now at the end of each block. The N's that were after an R are now before it. This is exactly a stable partition within each block: move all Ns before the R. Since there is only one R per block, it's just moving the R to the end.

Now, after this transformation, the new records are the left-to-right maxima of the resulting sequence. Since each block ends with an old R, and old R values are increasing, the old R's are records. The Ns at the start of each block may contain some new records (those that are > previous old R). But note the Ns within a block are in their original relative order. So the new records are the prefix maxima of the concatenation of blocks, which are: the first N of block1 (if any) and then some Ns, then old R1, then Ns of block2 that are > old R1, then old R2, etc. This is equivalent to taking the sequence of Ns from each block and merging them with the old R's based on value.

Maybe we can maintain the permutation as a sequence of values, and operation k is equivalent to:
- For each block, move the record to the end.
This is a stable partition of each block by whether element is the record. If we ignore values, the order of elements after operation is: for each block, all non-records in original order, then the record. This is exactly the same as the original sequence but with each record moved to the end of its block. This is a known operation: "move each left-to-right maximum to the end of its block".

Can we apply this to a treap by using a lazy tag on blocks? If we maintain the sequence as a list of blocks, and operation k applies a lazy "rotate block" to all blocks in prefix. But after rotation, the block boundaries (records) change because new records may appear inside the Ns. However, the old R's remain at the end of blocks; the new records are some Ns that are > previous R. These new records will become the starts of new blocks in the next operation. So the block decomposition after operation is: within each old block, after the Ns, there is the old R. The new records are the prefix maxima of the Ns (with threshold previous R). This means each old block may split into multiple blocks: the prefix maxima of its Ns become records, and the old R at the end is the last record of that old block. Thus the number of blocks can increase.

This is exactly like the Cartesian tree of the Ns. Maybe we can maintain a stack of records; operation k is like applying a "stack-sort" to the prefix.

Let's consider the algorithm "stack-sort" (Knuth): One pass of bubble sort from left to right is equivalent to: Use a stack, push elements, when? Actually left-to-right maxima can be found with a stack. Operation k moving records to end of blocks is similar to "pop and push".

Maybe we can process operations using a segment tree with "max" and "count of records" and a lazy tag "rotate blocks" that can be applied in O(log N) if we store for each node the result of applying one operation to its sequence? Let's revisit monoid.

For a sequence S, define F(S) = result of one bubble pass on S (operation |S|). We need apply F to prefix k. If we have a segment tree, we could apply F to a range by combining nodes. Is F decomposable? Let's find a monoid that supports concatenation and F.

Suppose we have two sequences A and B. We want F(A+B). Can we express F(A+B) in terms of F(A), F(B), and some summary of A/B? Let's simulate. One pass on A+B:
- First process A internally: A becomes F(A). Let a_max = max(A). After F(A), the last element of F(A) is a_max.
- Then compare a_max with first element of B. If a_max > B_1, swap; then a_max moves right through B until it encounters an element > a_max or end. This is equivalent to inserting a_max into B at the position before the first element greater than a_max, and shifting the preceding elements of B left by one (into the last position of A). The elements of B after the insertion point remain unchanged. Meanwhile, the part of B before the insertion point (all < a_max) moves left by one, and the last element of F(A) becomes the first of these shifted elements? Let's formalize.

Let B = C + D, where C is the longest prefix of B consisting of elements < a_max, and D starts with an element > a_max (or empty). Then after the boundary, a_max swaps with all elements of C, moving to the position before D (or end). The sequence becomes: F(A) with its last element a_max removed? Actually F(A) = A' + [a_max] (since F(A) ends with max). After a_max leaves, the last position of A part is filled by the first element of C? Let's trace [A=3,1, B=4,2], F(A)=[1,3], a_max=3. B C = [2]? Wait B=[4,2], first element 4 >3, so C empty, D=[4,2]. Boundary compare 3<4 no swap. Result F(A)+B = [1,3,4,2]. F(A+B) on [3,1,4,2] is [1,3,2,4], not [1,3,4,2]. Wait our decomposition: Process A internally: edges in A: [3,1] -> [1,3]. Then edge between A and B: compare 3 and4 no swap. Then process B internally: edges in B: [4,2] -> [2,4]. But the pass continues into B, so F(A+B) = [1,3] + F(B) = [1,3,2,4]. Yes! So if a_max < first(B), then after boundary no swap, and the rest of the pass is just F(B) on B. Thus F(A+B) = F(A) + F(B) if max(A) < first(B)? More generally, if a_max < all elements of B? Actually if a_max < first(B), then no swap at boundary, and a_max never enters B; the pass inside B is exactly F(B). Since a_max < first(B) and first(B) is the first element of B, but a_max could be greater than later elements of B; however, a_max is at the end of A, and the boundary compare is with first(B). If a_max < first(B), no swap, so a_max stays in A, and the pass continues with edge between first(B) and second(B), which is exactly F(B) on B (because B is processed from its start). If max(A) > first(B), then a_max swaps with first(B) and continues into B. This is more complex.

Operation l processes edges 1..l-1. In a compare-exchange network, the final state depends on the order of edge processing, not just counts. But for bubble sort with this specific order (left-to-right passes with increasing boundary), maybe the state after a sequence can be determined by counts c_j = number of operations with A_i ≥ j+1. Because the schedule is "stable": edge j is processed after edge j-1 in each pass, and the number of passes over edge j is c_j. This is exactly the "Bubble Sort" network where each edge has a certain number of activations. There is a known result: In a sorting network that is a sequence of left-to-right bubble passes, the final position of each element depends on the number of passes, and can be computed by a "patience sorting" algorithm.

Let's define for each operation t with value k, it processes edges 1..k-1. So edge i is processed in operations where k ≥ i+1. Since A non-decreasing, let c_i = #{t : A_t ≥ i+1}. Then edge i is processed exactly c_i times, and the order is: for each operation t, process edges 1..A_t-1 in increasing order. This is equivalent to running a bubble sort where edge i is activated c_i times, with the constraint that in each "round" t, edges up to A_t-1 are activated in order. Since A_t non-decreasing, the set of active edges expands.

So new element at position i is old element at position i+1, except at block ends where it is the old record at block start.

The new record status at position i is whether this new element is greater than all new elements before it. This is complex.

Need a more clever aggregate.

Notice in the pattern above, operation N repeatedly on the same prefix. Repeated operation N is just standard bubble sort passes. There are known formulas for inversion count after c passes: It decreases by sum of something. But we need arbitrary A, not just repeated N. For a fixed array, the inversion count after c bubble sort passes is known: It equals sum over elements of max(0, something). There is a formula using the "bubble sort distance" (number of passes needed to move each element to its sorted position). Specifically, after c passes, the inversion count is sum_{i} max(0, L_i - c)? Let's test.

So for binary, after c passes, inversion count = sum over 0s max(0, ones_before - c). This is known.

For a permutation of distinct values, inversion count can be decomposed by thresholds: inv(P) = sum_{v=1}^{N-1} inv_binary_v, where binary_v marks elements > v. After c passes of bubble sort on the whole array, the binary sequence for each threshold undergoes c passes. This is the key solution! Let's formalize:

- Let L[v] = initial inversion contribution of value v: number of elements greater than v that appear before v in the initial permutation. Initially, inv = sum L[v].
- For each operation k, because A is non-decreasing, the set of values currently in the first k positions is exactly the set of values initially in the first k positions. Operation k is a bubble sort pass on this set. In a bubble sort pass, for every element in the prefix with L>0, L decreases by exactly 1; elements with L=0 stay 0. Elements outside the prefix are unaffected.
- Therefore, processing operation k is equivalent to: For each value v whose initial position ≤ k and L[v] > 0, decrement L[v] by 1. The inversion count after the operation is the new sum of L.
- Since A is non-decreasing, k values are processed in non-decreasing order. We need to perform these decrements efficiently and output sum after each operation.

Now the problem reduces to: We have N items (values) indexed by their initial position pos0[v] (1..N), each with a positive integer L[v] (could be 0). We process queries in non-decreasing k. Each query k: for all items with pos0 ≤ k and L>0, decrement L by1. Output sum of L.

This is much simpler! We don't need to update positions or permutation at all. The L values only decrease, and the set of affected items for query k is a prefix of initial positions [1..k]. Since k is non-decreasing, the affected prefix expands to the right over time. But note queries with same k repeated affect the same prefix [1..k] multiple times. We need to decrement all positive L in that prefix each time.

We need a data structure to support:
- Range prefix [1..k]: for all items with L>0, decrement L by1.
- Query total sum of L.

This is a classic "range decrement positive" on a static array of initial positions. Since k is non-decreasing, the range [1..k] only expands. But repeated queries with same k apply to the same range multiple times. We can process by grouping identical k? However, we need output after each query, so if c queries with same k, we need perform c decrements on prefix [1..k] and output after each.

We need efficient O((N+M) log N) or similar.

Data structure options:
1. Segment tree beats supporting range add -1 to positive elements (i.e., L = max(0, L-1)) and sum query. Since L values are up to N, and total decrements over all elements is sum initial L = initial inv ≤ N(N-1)/2 ~2e10, but M=2e5. A segment tree beats for range chmax (L = max(0, L-1)) can handle range decrement positive in O(log N) amortized? Actually operation "decrement all positive values in range by 1" is equivalent to L = max(0, L-1). This is range add -1 followed by chmax 0. Segment tree beats supports range add and range chmax? We can maintain minimum and second minimum? Since we only need to decrement positive values, we can maintain the minimum positive value in the range. If min positive > 1, we can lazily add -1 to all elements in the range (including zeros? But zeros should not be decremented. If we add -1 to all, zeros become -1, wrong. So we need to exclude zeros. We can maintain minimum value in range. If min > 0, apply -1 to all. If min == 0, we cannot apply to all; we need to recurse to avoid zeros. This is like segment tree beats for range decrement with lower bound 0. Complexity can be O((N+M) log N) amortized if we maintain min and max? Let's analyze.

We have an array L[1..N] (indexed by initial position). Operation: for i in [1..k], if L[i]>0: L[i]--. This is equivalent to L[i] = max(0, L[i]-1). This is a range "saturating decrement". There is a known segment tree beats technique: maintain minimum value mn and second minimum? Actually for range add and range chmax (a_i = max(a_i, x)), segment tree beats can do O(log N) amortized. Here we want a_i = max(0, a_i - 1), which is range add -1 then chmax 0. We can implement a segment tree that supports range add and range chmax (lower bound) using segment tree beats. But maybe simpler due to static and prefix queries.

Since k is non-decreasing, the range [1..k] only expands. However, repeated queries with same k apply to the same range. We can process each element's L independently: Each element at initial position i is decremented once for every operation with k ≥ i, until its L reaches 0. Because operation k affects all positions ≤k. Since A is non-decreasing, the number of operations with k ≥ i is simply the count of queries from the first time k ≥ i to the end. Let c_i = number of operations with A_t ≥ i. Then element i will be decremented min(L_i, c_i) times over the entire sequence. But we need output after each operation, not just final. We can compute the total decrements up to each operation t: For operation t with value k, it decrements all elements i ≤ k that still have L_i > 0. The total decrement in this operation is the number of elements i ≤ k with current L_i > 0. That's exactly k - R_k, where R_k is number of zeros in prefix [1..k] (records). We are back to R_k, but now R_k is just count of L_i=0 among initial positions ≤k. Wait L_i is associated with initial position i, not current position. Operation k decrements positive L_i for i≤k. So the number of decrements in operation k is count of i≤k with L_i>0. This is k - (# zeros among L[1..k]). This matches earlier R_k? Initially, L_i=0 iff value at initial position i is a record? Actually L_v=0 for record values, but indexed by value, not position. Now we index by initial position i. The value at initial position i has some L. The number of zeros among L[1..k] is the number of values in the initial prefix that are records (L=0). That is exactly initial records in prefix k? But after operations, L changes; zeros can increase. So R_k dynamic.

We need process queries and output sum. We can maintain an array A[i] = L of the value initially at position i. Operation k: for i=1..k, A[i] = max(0, A[i]-1). Sum decreases by count of positive A[i] in [1..k]. We need output sum after each.

This is a range decrement-positive on a static prefix. Since k non-decreasing, we can use a Fenwick tree over positions to maintain positive counts and sums, and a priority queue / DSU to skip zeros? Let's think.

Each operation k decrements all positive elements in prefix [1..k]. If we process operations one by one, we need to decrement many elements. But each element's A[i] decreases by1 for each operation with k≥i until zero. Total decrements over all elements = initial sum L = initial inversion count, which can be O(N^2) ~2e10, too large. But M=2e5, so total decrements we actually need to apply is at most M * N? No, each operation can decrement many elements, but we can aggregate using lazy tags.

Since k is non-decreasing, we can maintain a global "time" or "number of operations applied to prefix". For each position i, it is affected by all operations with k ≥ i. Let t_i = number of operations processed so far with k ≥ i. Then the current A[i] = max(0, initial_A[i] - t_i). Because each such operation decrements it by1 until zero. Is that true? Yes, since operation k affects all positions ≤k, and k non-decreasing, the set of operations affecting position i is exactly those with k ≥ i. They occur in a contiguous suffix of the operation sequence (because once k reaches i, it stays ≥i). Thus t_i is simply the number of operations processed so far minus the number of operations before the first k≥i. Let's define for each position i, start_i = first index t such that A_t ≥ i. Then after processing q operations, t_i = max(0, q - start_i + 1). Then A[i] = max(0, initial_A[i] - t_i). This is a closed form! We don't need to simulate decrements per operation. We can compute the sum after each operation q as:
Sum(q) = ∑_{i=1}^N max(0, initial_A[i] - max(0, q - start_i + 1)).
Where start_i = min{t : A_t ≥ i}, or ∞ if no operation with k≥i (then t_i=0).

Because A is non-decreasing, start_i is non-increasing with i? Actually larger i requires larger k, so start_i is later (larger index) for larger i. Since A_t non-decreasing, the first time A_t ≥ i is non-decreasing in i. Let's verify: If i increases, threshold increases, so first occurrence is later or never. So start_i is non-decreasing.

We need output Sum(q) for q=1..M. This is now a simple formula! Let's test with sample.

i1:2, A1=0
i2:2, A2=0
i3:2, A3=0
i4:2, A4=max(0,3-2)=1

Sum=1. Output1. Matches.

Sample2 we can trust.

This is the ultimate simplification! Let's prove the formula carefully.

For each initial position i (1-indexed), let a_i = L_{P_i} = number of larger elements before P_i in the initial permutation. This is the inversion contribution of the element at position i. Initially inv = ∑ a_i.

Because A is non-decreasing, for any operation with value k, the set of elements in positions 1..k is exactly the initial set of positions 1..k. Operation k is a bubble sort pass on this set. In one bubble sort pass on a sequence, for every element in the sequence, its "number of larger elements before it" decreases by 1 if positive, and stays 0 if already 0. Elements outside the sequence are unaffected. Therefore, after processing a prefix of operations, the value a_i for the element initially at position i has been decremented once for every operation with k ≥ i (since those operations include position i in their prefix), until it reaches 0. Operations with k < i do not affect it. Since A is non-decreasing, the operations with k ≥ i form a suffix of the processed operations. Thus after q operations, the number of decrements applied to position i is d_i(q) = max(0, q - s_i + 1), where s_i is the first operation index with A_{s_i} ≥ i; if no such operation, d_i=0. Then current a_i = max(0, initial_a_i - d_i(q)). The inversion count after q operations is ∑ current a_i.

We can compute s_i efficiently:
Given sorted A length M. For each i from 1 to N, s_i = lower_bound(A, i) (first index with A >= i), 1-based; if not found, s_i = M+1 (or INF). Since A_i ≥2, for i=1, s_1=1 always (because A_1≥2≥1). For i>N none.

Then for each query q from 1..M, we need compute:
Sum(q) = ∑_{i=1}^N max(0, a_i - max(0, q - s_i + 1)).
Let's simplify. For a given q, positions i with s_i ≤ q have d_i = q - s_i + 1; others d_i=0. Since s_i is non-decreasing in i, the set of i with s_i ≤ q is a prefix of positions: i ≤ L_q, where L_q = max i such that s_i ≤ q. But s_i = first A_t ≥ i. Since A_t non-decreasing, s_i ≤ q iff A_q ≥ i (because if by operation q the threshold has reached i). Indeed, s_i ≤ q ⇔ there exists t≤q with A_t ≥ i ⇔ A_q ≥ i (since A non-decreasing, max up to q is A_q). Thus the affected positions after q operations are exactly i ≤ A_q! Wait that's obvious: operation q has value k=A_q, and it affects positions ≤k. But earlier operations also affected positions ≤ their k ≤ A_q. So after q operations, positions i ≤ A_q have been affected at least once; positions >A_q never affected. More precisely, d_i(q) = number of operations t≤q with A_t ≥ i. Since A_t non-decreasing, this is q - s_i + 1 for i ≤ A_q, and 0 for i > A_q. And s_i is first t with A_t ≥ i.

So Sum(q) = ∑_{i=1}^{A_q} max(0, a_i - (q - s_i + 1)) + ∑_{i=A_q+1}^N a_i.
Because positions > A_q have d=0.

We need compute this sum for each q efficiently. N,M up to 2e5. We can precompute s_i and then for each q compute sum over i≤A_q of max(0, a_i - (q - s_i + 1)).

Let b_i = a_i + s_i - 1? Then a_i - (q - s_i + 1) = a_i + s_i - 1 - q. Let c_i = a_i + s_i - 1. Then contribution of position i at query q (if i≤A_q) is max(0, c_i - q). For i>A_q, contribution is a_i (since d=0). But note if i≤A_q, s_i ≤ q, so c_i - q = a_i - d_i. If a_i - d_i could be negative, max0.

Thus Sum(q) = ∑_{i=1}^{A_q} max(0, c_i - q) + ∑_{i=A_q+1}^N a_i.

Since q increases, and A_q non-decreasing. We can process queries offline with a Fenwick tree over positions? Or over values c_i.

We need for each q:
- Let k = A_q.
- Sum over i=1..k of max(0, c_i - q).
- Plus suffix sum of a_i for i>k.

We can precompute prefix sums of a_i to get suffix sum in O(1): suffixA[k+1] = totalA - prefixA[k].

For the first part, we need query over positions i≤k of max(0, c_i - q). This is a 2D query: among i≤k, sum of (c_i - q) for those with c_i > q. Equivalently:
∑_{i≤k, c_i>q} c_i - q * count_{i≤k, c_i>q}.

We can process q from 1 to M, maintaining a data structure over positions i that have become "active" (c_i > current q?) Actually q increases, so threshold q increases. We need for each query (k,q) count and sum of c_i among i≤k with c_i>q.

This is a standard offline 2D dominance query. N,M 2e5. We can sort positions by c_i descending and queries by q descending, then use a Fenwick tree over positions to add c_i and count. But queries have k=A_q, and q is the query index. We can process q from M down to1? Let's see.

For each query q, we need among i≤k with c_i > q. If we process q decreasing, we add positions with c_i > q as q decreases. Then query prefix k for count and sum. This works.

Algorithm:
- Compute a_i for i=1..N.
- Compute s_i = lower_bound(A, i) (1-based index), or INF = M+1 if no A_t ≥ i. For i > maxA, s_i = INF. But note for i > A_q, they are not in the first part; suffix sum handles them. In the 2D query, k=A_q ≤ maxA, so i≤k always have s_i ≤ M (since A has some ≥i? If i≤maxA, yes s_i exists).
- c_i = a_i + s_i - 1. Since s_i can be M+1 for i>maxA, but those i never appear in i≤k for any query because k≤maxA. So we can ignore or set c_i large.
- totalA = sum a_i.
- prefixA array for suffix sum.

For each query q (1..M):
   k = A[q]
   ans = suffix_sum_after_k + query_sum(k, q)
where query_sum(k,q) = sum_{i≤k, c_i>q} (c_i - q).

Process q from M down to1:
   Maintain BIT_count and BIT_sum over positions i.
   We need to add all positions i with c_i > q. Since q decreases, we can sort positions by c_i descending. Start with pointer at positions with c_i > M? Actually q max M. For q from M down to1, before answering query q, add all i with c_i > q. Then query prefix k.
   But note c_i can be >M? a_i up to N, s_i up to M, so c_i up to N+M ~4e5. We can sort by c_i.
   For q from M down to1:
       while ptr < N and sorted_by_c[ptr].c > q:
           add position i to BITs (count+1, sum+c_i)
       cnt = BIT_count.query(k)
       sumc = BIT_sum.query(k)
       part1 = sumc - q * cnt
       ans = suffixA[k+1] + part1
       output ans for query q (but queries output in original order; if we process descending, store ans[q]).

Wait, for q=M, we add positions with c_i > M. Then query k=A_M. For q=M-1, add positions with c_i > M-1 (which includes those >M plus those ==M? Actually c_i > M-1 means c_i ≥ M, so add c_i=M if any). Good.

Now we need compute a_i (initial L for value at position i). We can compute L_v for each value using a Fenwick tree over values:
Scan P left to right. For value x at position i, a_i = number of previous elements greater than x = (i-1) - bit.sum(x). Then add x to BIT. This gives a_i directly. Also total inv = sum a_i.

Compute s_i:
Given A (0-indexed or 1-indexed). For each i from 1 to N, find first index t (1-based) with A_t >= i. Since A sorted non-decreasing. We can use bisect_left on A (0-based) +1. If i > A[-1], s_i = INF (M+1). But for i≤maxA, s_i exists.
We only need s_i for i up to maxA maybe, because queries k≤maxA. For i>maxA, they never appear in prefix k, and suffix sum includes their a_i always. In the 2D query, positions i>maxA are never queried (k≤maxA), so we can ignore them in BIT. But suffixA[k+1] includes their a_i. So we can set c_i = -inf for i>maxA or just not add them to sorted list. For i≤maxA, s_i = bisect_left(A, i)+1.

c_i = a_i + s_i - 1.

Now process queries:
- We need answer for each q in original order. We can compute ans array of length M.
- Sort positions i (1..maxA) by c_i descending. But if c_i can be negative? a_i≥0, s_i≥1, so c_i≥0. For q from M down to1, add positions with c_i > q.
- Fenwick trees over positions (1..N) for count and sum of c_i. Since queries ask prefix k, positions are original indices i. We add at index i.
- For q from M down to1:
    while ptr < len(items) and items[ptr].c > q:
        bit_count.add(i,1)
        bit_sum.add(i,c)
        ptr++
    k = A[q-1] (if 0-indexed)
    cnt = bit_count.sum(k)
    sumc = bit_sum.sum(k)
    part1 = sumc - q * cnt
    ans[q-1] = (totalA - prefixA[k]) + part1
Where prefixA[k] = sum_{i=1}^k a_i. suffix = totalA - prefixA[k].
Note: If k > N? A_i ≤ N, so k≤N.

Let's test edge cases:
- M operations, some A_i = 2. s_i for i=1,2 =1. For i>2 maybe later.
- If a_i=0, c_i = s_i-1. It may be >q and added to BIT, but contribution max(0, c_i - q) = max(0, s_i-1 - q). Since s_i≤q for i≤A_q, s_i-1 - q ≤ -1, so contribution 0. Wait if a_i=0, c_i = s_i-1. For query q≥s_i, c_i - q ≤ -1, max0=0. In our offline processing, we add positions with c_i > q. For a_i=0, c_i = s_i-1. Since for i≤A_q, s_i≤q, c_i≤q-1, so not added. Good. If i>A_q, not in prefix. So zeros don't contribute. Nice.

- What about positions i with s_i = M+1 (never affected)? They have i>maxA. They are not in any prefix k (since k≤maxA), so their a_i remains in suffix sum. We don't add them to BIT. Good.

- If A has duplicates, s_i lower_bound works. For i≤A_q, s_i≤q. c_i computed with first occurrence. The number of decrements d_i(q)=q-s_i+1. If there are duplicates, once k reaches i, every subsequent operation including duplicates decrements. Formula uses q - s_i +1, correct.

Let's verify with a case where A starts at 3, so position1,2 not affected until q=1? Actually A_1=3, so s_1=s_2=s_3=1. Operation3 affects positions1..3, so yes.

Now, is the set invariance proof fully correct for all operations? We used that before operation k, previous operations have values ≤k, so they don't change the set of positions 1..k. Since A non-decreasing, yes. Thus operation k affects exactly initial positions 1..k. But wait, previous operations with value = k (duplicates) also don't change set. So at any operation with value k, the set of values in positions 1..k is initial set. Good.

Now, does operation k decrement L for all positive elements in the current prefix, where L is the current inversion contribution? We proved for a bubble pass on a sequence, each element's number of larger elements before it decreases by1 if positive. Let's prove: In one left-to-right bubble pass on a sequence, for each element x, let c(x) be the number of elements greater than x before x. After the pass, c'(x) = max(0, c(x)-1). This is a known property. We can prove by considering the largest element? Let's verify with [4,1,3,2]: c for values: 1:1,2:2,3:1,4:0. After pass [1,3,2,4]: c' 1:0,2:1,3:0,4:0. Yes. This property holds for bubble sort pass. Good.

Thus the solution is solid.

Let's outline final code:

1. Read N, P list.
2. Compute a array of length N (1-indexed):
   BIT over values 1..N.
   For idx, x in enumerate(P, start=1):
       less_or_equal = bit_sum(x)
       greater_before = (idx-1) - less_or_equal
       a[idx] = greater_before
       bit_add(x,1)
   total_inv = sum(a)
3. Read M, A list.
4. Compute prefixA: pref[0]=0; for i in 1..N: pref[i]=pref[i-1]+a[i].
5. Compute s_i for i=1..N:
   Use bisect_left from bisect module on A (0-indexed). Since A sorted.
   For i in 1..N:
       pos = bisect_left(A, i)  # 0-based index
       if pos < M: s_i = pos+1
       else: s_i = M+1 (or INF)
   But we only need for i≤maxA. We can loop i=1..maxA maybe. For i>maxA, no need. However, prefixA includes all.
   maxA = A[-1].
   items = []
   for i in range(1, maxA+1):
       s = bisect_left(A, i) + 1  # since i≤maxA, exists
       c = a[i] + s - 1
       items.append((c, i))
   Sort items by c descending.
6. Fenwick trees:
   bit_cnt = Fenwick(N)
   bit_sum = Fenwick(N)  # stores c values, can be large up to N+M ~4e5, int fine.
7. ans = [0]*M
   ptr=0
   for q in range(M, 0, -1):
       while ptr < len(items) and items[ptr][0] > q:
           c, i = items[ptr]
           bit_cnt.add(i,1)
           bit_sum.add(i,c)
           ptr += 1
       k = A[q-1]
       cnt = bit_cnt.sum(k)
       sumc = bit_sum.sum(k)
       part1 = sumc - q * cnt
       suffix = total_inv - pref[k]  # pref[k] sum a[1..k]
       ans[q-1] = suffix + part1
8. Print ans lines.

Need ensure total_inv - pref[k] is nonnegative. Yes.

Great.

Now, one subtlety: The a_i we compute is initial L for the value at initial position i. But when we index by initial position, operation k decrements a_i for i≤k. Is it possible that an element initially at position i≤k has already been decremented by previous operations with value <k? Yes, and our formula accounts via s_i. But the property "operation k decrements all positive a_i in initial prefix k" holds for the current a_i, not initial. Since previous operations with value <k also affected position i (if k_prev ≥ i). The total number of decrements up to q is q - s_i +1. This is correct because every operation with value ≥i decrements it once. Since A non-decreasing, once value reaches i, all subsequent operations have value ≥i. So yes.

Let's prove the "every operation with k≥i decrements position i once" from the bubble pass property: Operation k is a pass on the set of initial positions 1..k. For an element in that set, its current L decreases by1 if positive. If L=0, stays0. So if we count decrements, each operation with k≥i attempts to decrement; if L already0, no change. The formula max(0, a_i - d) handles saturation. Good.

Now, complexity:
- Computing a: O(N log N)
- Computing s_i: O(maxA log M) using bisect (O(log M) each) or two-pointer O(N+M) because A sorted. N=2e5, bisect fine.
- Sorting items O(N log N).
- Processing queries O((N+M) log N) with Fenwick.

Total O((N+M) log N), good.

Let's consider memory: Fenwick arrays size N+2, items size maxA≤N.

Implementation details for Fenwick:
```
class BIT:
    def __init__(self,n):
        self.n=n
        self.bit=[0]*(n+1)
    def add(self,i,v):
        while i<=self.n:
            self.bit[i]+=v
            i+=i&-i
    def sum(self,i):
        s=0
        while i>0:
            s+=self.bit[i]
            i-=i&-i
        return s
```
Use 1-based positions.

Computing a:
```
bit = BIT(N)
a = [0]*(N+1)
for idx,x in enumerate(P,1):
    le = bit.sum(x)
    a[idx] = (idx-1) - le
    bit.add(x,1)
total = sum(a)
```
Note: bit.sum(x) counts previous elements ≤x. Since permutation, previous elements greater than x = (idx-1) - previous elements ≤x. Correct.

prefix:
```
pref=[0]*(N+1)
for i in range(1,N+1):
    pref[i]=pref[i-1]+a[i]
```

s_i:
```
from bisect import bisect_left
maxA = A[-1]
items=[]
for i in range(1, maxA+1):
    s = bisect_left(A, i) + 1  # A 0-indexed
    c = a[i] + s - 1
    items.append((c, i))
items.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)
```
If maxA could be <1? A_i≥2, so maxA≥2.

Process:
```
bit_cnt=BIT(N)
bit_sum=BIT(N)
ans=[0]*M
ptr=0
for q in range(M,0,-1):
    while ptr < len(items) and items[ptr][0] > q:
        c,i = items[ptr]
        bit_cnt.add(i,1)
        bit_sum.add(i,c)
        ptr += 1
    k = A[q-1]
    cnt = bit_cnt.sum(k)
    sumc = bit_sum.sum(k)
    part1 = sumc - q * cnt
    suffix = total - pref[k]
    ans[q-1] = suffix + part1
```
Need ensure part1 is int; q*cnt up to 2e5*2e5=4e10, Python int fine.

```
def main():

    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it)
    P = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]
    M = next(it)
    A = [next(it) for _ in range(M)]
    ...
```

Fenwick class as nested functions for speed? Use local variables.

Compute a:
```
bit = [0]*(N+1)
def add(i,v):
    while i<=N:
        bit[i]+=v
        i += i & -i
def sum_(i):
    s=0
    while i>0:
        s+=bit[i]
        i -= i & -i
    return s
a=[0]*(N+1)
for idx,x in enumerate(P,1):
    le = sum_(x)
    a[idx] = (idx-1) - le
    add(x,1)
total = sum(a)
```

prefix:
```
pref=[0]*(N+1)
for i in range(1,N+1):
    pref[i]=pref[i-1]+a[i]
```

items:
```
maxA = A[-1]
items=[]
for i in range(1, maxA+1):
    s = bisect_left(A, i) + 1
    c = a[i] + s - 1
    items.append((c, i))
items.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)
```

Fenwick for processing:
```
bit_cnt=[0]*(N+1)
bit_sum=[0]*(N+1)
def add_cnt(i,v):
    while i<=N:
        bit_cnt[i]+=v
        i += i&-i
...
```
Could define a generic BIT class but functions faster.

Processing:
```
ans=[0]*M
ptr=0
for q in range(M,0,-1):
    while ptr < len(items) and items[ptr][0] > q:
        c,i = items[ptr]
        # add to BITs
        j=i
        while j<=N:
            bit_cnt[j]+=1
            bit_sum[j]+=c
            j += j&-j
        ptr += 1
    k = A[q-1]
    cnt=0; sumc=0
    j=k
    while j>0:
        cnt += bit_cnt[j]
        sumc += bit_sum[j]
        j -= j&-j
    part1 = sumc - q * cnt
    suffix = total - pref[k]
    ans[q-1] = suffix + part1
```

Current a_i = max(0, a_i - d_i). Contribution to inv = max(0, a_i - d_i) = max(0, a_i - (q - s_i +1)) = max(0, (a_i + s_i -1) - q) = max(0, c_i - q). For i≤k=A_q, s_i≤q, so d_i = q-s_i+1. For i>k, s_i>q (since A_q < i), so d_i=0, contribution a_i. Thus sum = ∑_{i≤k} max(0,c_i-q) + ∑_{i>k} a_i. Our offline query uses positions i≤k with c_i>q. Correct.

Now, let's think about the initial a_i: It is the number of larger elements before the value at initial position i. For a bubble pass on the prefix, the property "c(x) decreases by1" applies to the current sequence, where c(x) is number of larger elements before x in the current sequence. We are tracking c for each initial position i. Since the set of positions in prefix k is initial positions 1..k, and larger elements before a position within the prefix are also within the prefix, c(x) within the prefix equals its global c(x). After operation, global c(x) decreases by1. This holds even if x is not in prefix? For x outside prefix, global c(x) unchanged because no element crosses boundary? Wait operation k swaps within prefix k, so no element crosses boundary k. For an element x outside prefix (position >k), all elements before it consist of prefix k (same set) and positions k+1..before. The set of prefix k is unchanged, so the number of larger elements before x from prefix k is unchanged. Thus c(x) unchanged. Good. This addresses earlier concern.

Now, one more subtlety: The operation k is a bubble pass on the current prefix, but the current prefix set is initial positions 1..k. The current order of these elements may have been changed by previous operations with smaller k. The property c decreases by1 holds for any sequence after one bubble pass, regardless of initial order. So yes.

But before final, let's revisit the proof of "operation k decrements L for all positive elements in prefix" one more time to be absolutely certain. We rely on the known property of bubble sort pass. Let's prove quickly:

In a sequence, define c_x = # elements greater than x before x. In one left-to-right bubble pass, for each element x:
- If c_x = 0, x is a left-to-right maximum. It moves right across some smaller elements, but no larger element before it, so after pass c'_x = 0.
- If c_x > 0, let y be the nearest larger element to the left of x? Actually x will be swapped left by exactly one larger element? In a bubble pass, each element with c_x>0 moves left by one position, and the number of larger elements before it decreases by exactly1. This is a standard property. We can accept.

Now, one subtlety: Operation k is a pass on the prefix, but the "larger elements before x" for x in prefix might include elements outside prefix? No, if x in prefix, any element before x is also in prefix. So global c_x = c_x within prefix. Good.

Now, the set of elements in prefix k is initial positions 1..k because previous operations have values ≤k. This relies on A non-decreasing. If A had a larger value before a smaller, not true. But constraints ensure.

Now, let's consider if A has value k repeated, and between repetitions no larger operations. The set remains initial prefix k. Each repetition decrements positive L again. Formula d_i counts each operation with A_t ≥ i, including duplicates. Good.

Now, final code.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
from bisect import bisect_left


def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)

    N = next(it)
    P = [next(it) for _ in range(N)]
    M = next(it)
    A = [next(it) for _ in range(M)]

    # a[i] = initial inversion contribution of the element initially at position i
    bit = [0] * (N + 1)

    def bit_add(i, v):
        while i <= N:
            bit[i] += v
            i += i & -i

    def bit_sum(i):
        s = 0
        while i > 0:
            s += bit[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return s

    a = [0] * (N + 1)
    for idx, x in enumerate(P, 1):
        le = bit_sum(x)
        a[idx] = (idx - 1) - le
        bit_add(x, 1)

    total_inv = sum(a)

    pref = [0] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        pref[i] = pref[i - 1] + a[i]

    maxA = A[-1]

    # s_i = first operation index (1-based) with A_s >= i
    # c_i = a_i + s_i - 1
    items = []
    for i in range(1, maxA + 1):
        s = bisect_left(A, i) + 1
        c = a[i] + s - 1
        items.append((c, i))

    items.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

    bit_cnt = [0] * (N + 1)
    bit_sum_c = [0] * (N + 1)

    def add_item(i, c):
        while i <= N:
            bit_cnt[i] += 1
            bit_sum_c[i] += c
            i += i & -i

    def query_prefix(i):
        cnt = 0
        sm = 0
        while i > 0:
            cnt += bit_cnt[i]
            sm += bit_sum_c[i]
            i -= i & -i
        return cnt, sm

    ans = [0] * M
    ptr = 0

    for q in range(M, 0, -1):
        while ptr < len(items) and items[ptr][0] > q:
            c, i = items[ptr]
            add_item(i, c)
            ptr += 1

        k = A[q - 1]
        cnt, sm = query_prefix(k)

        # sum_{i<=k, c_i>q} (c_i - q)
        part1 = sm - q * cnt

        # elements initially after position k are unaffected
        suffix = total_inv - pref[k]

        ans[q - 1] = suffix + part1

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(map(str, ans)))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
